
This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project 
to make the world's books discoverable online. 

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject 
to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books 
are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that's often difficult to discover. 

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file - a reminder of this book's long journey from the 
publisher to a library and finally to you. 

Usage guidelines 

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the 
public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to 
prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on automated querying. 

We also ask that you: 

+ Make non-commercial use ofthefiles We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for 
personal, non-commercial purposes. 

+ Refrainfrom automated querying Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google's system: If you are conducting research on machine 
translation, optical character recognition or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the 
use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help. 

+ Maintain attribution The Google "watermark" you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find 
additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it. 

+ Keep it legal Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just 
because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other 
countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can't offer guidance on whether any specific use of 
any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book's appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner 
anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe. 

About Google Book Search 

Google's mission is to organize the world's information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers 
discover the world's books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. You can search through the full text of this book on the web

At a Meeting of the Council of the Surtees Society 
on the 6th day of September, 1844, 

Resolved, That the Bursar^s Book in the Excuequbr 
OF DuRHAM be one of the Surtees Society^s Publications 
for the year 1844, and that the Secretary be requested to 
edit the same on the usual terms. 

James Raine, Secretary.

PREFACE. 



THIS volume consists of an accurate transcript of 
the original priyate Account Book of Robert Benett^ 
Bursar of the Priory of Durham for the period of four 
years, from Whitsuntide 1530^ to Whitsuntide 1534. 
One gathering of paper in the first year, consisting 
apparently of about twenty leaves, is unfortunately want- 
ing. The book is of a small quarto size, in a wrapper 
of parchment without boards^ and is preserved'in the 
office of the auditor of the Bishop of Durham.* 

Other books of the same character, more or less per- 
fect, are in the custody of the Dean and Chapter of 
Durham; but, as they contain more of receipt.than 
expenditure, each book being preceded by a rental, 
which is the same in every year, and few of them enter- 
ing into much detail with respect to purchases or pay- 

* In the book is a loose letter-back, containing references to some of 
its contents, in the handwriting of the person to whom it is addressed, — 
" To his woorthy fi^inde M' John Richardson, at his house in Durham, 
these dd." Now, as John Richardson held an office in the Exchequer, 
under the Bishop of Durham, in the earlier part of the seventeenth cen-* 
tury, it is probable that the book has been preseived in its present place 
of custody at least since his period.

Vlll PREFACE. 

ments^ they are not of so interesting a nature as that 
which is now presented ta the members of the Society. 
They have, however, as will be seen from the Glossary 
appended to these pages, afforded many explanations of 
terms used in the present book, and many very valu- 
able illustrations. 

The nature of the book must next be described. The 
Bursar was the chief officer of the monastery, under the 
Prior, having under him all the other officers {ohedietV' 
tiarii) of the house, each of whom exhibited to him 
yearly, at Pentecost, an account of his receipts and 
expenses in his peculiar office. To the sacrist, for 
instance, were appropriated certain tithes and revenues 
to defray the expenses incidental to the performance of 
divine service in the church, the purchase of robes, and 
the repairs of the fabric. Every other department had 
its respective head, but all accounted to the Bursar;* 
and, when their various yearly account rolls had been 
laid before him, and had been duly examined and 
audited, one large roll was yearly compiled, called the 

* " The Bowcer*s office was to receave all the rentes that was per- 
te^ming to the house, and all other officers of the house mayde there 
accoumptes to him, and he discharged all the servants' wages, and paid 
all the expences and sommes of money as was laid furth about any work 
apperteining to the said Abey, or that the house was charged with all." 
— The Monastical Church ofDurhanty p. 83.

PREFAGB. IX 

Bursar's RoU, embod^dng the whole proceedings of the 
monastery in a summary way^ and was, with the rolls 
of the inferior offieers upon which it was founded, duly 
deposited in the treasury or muniment room of the 
conyent. The Bursar's Bboks here printed contain the 
most minute details of his own proceedings as Bursar 
during their respective years, and their substance was 
duly transferr6d to his great annual account roll — ^but 
their substance bnly. In the roll he contents himself 
with stating in general terms his expenditure under its 
respective heads ; but here in his book we have daily 
memoranda under every department within his care, 
with the most minute specification of names and prices, 
together with numerous other entries to aid his me- 
mory and guide him in his propeedings. In addition 
to much voluntary and valuable information of this 
nature, the flyJeaves at the beginning and end of 
each year abound with the most curious entries, which 
the Bursar intended for no eye save his own ; but the^e 
entries do not constitute the chief value of his book. 
We have here every article of food and clothing, the 
whole consumption in short of the great monastery of 
Durham for four years, most minutely detailed. Men 
and their wages, prices and quantities, necessaries and 
luxuries, are all set forth to the inquisitive reader by a

RECEPTIO SPECIERUM A MAGISTRO SWYNBURNE 
DE NOVO CASTRO a festo Omnium Sanctorum in anno

EXPLANATION OF WORDS. 



A. 

AI.I.ECE3 Albi. Dried herrings, retain- 
ing their white colour. " 1539-40. Paid 
to William Blythman and Ralph Bow- 
nuui for the carriage of 1000 white 
herrings, and two loads of * poudert * 
fiflh with four honefl, at 6d, each, 2«.'* 
— Bur9ar'*i Book. 
Au^ES So&i. Dried herrings of a red 
colour. The " poudert " fish, or fiah 
red with salt, mentioned under the 
preceding word. See SoRUS. 
Almayne Reyvetts, 139. Defensive suits 
of armour for the body, composed 
of splints rivetted together. Kempe, 
They are here called Alniayiie or Qer- 
man, perfaapa firom the country in which 
they were chiefly used, or from which 
they were introduced into England. 

Ampnvs or UM. A corruption of amnis, 
a river. JUparacio Ampni, p. 280, 
&c. the repairing of the mill dam at the 
west end of the cathedral. Tlie dam 
was made of stone, and the crevices 
were stuffed with moss, of which large 
quantities were procured every year 
for this purpose. It was generally 
gathered and broitght to the dam by 
girls. 

Anoell Nobles, 825. The gold coin or 
noble, so called from the angel repre- 
sented on one of its sides. 

Annes. Anniseeds. 

AppRENTicius. An apprentice. " Received 
of the Sacrist for the table (or board) 
of the apprentice of tho Lord Prior, 
29s. Sd.''—Burmr's Book for 1517. 

AquA DuLCis. Fresh water, in opposi- 
tion to salt water or the sea. Pisces 
aquce dulcis, fish from rivers or brooks. 
The Brownie is frequently called "aqua 
dulcis de Brun/* 



Aries, p. 29, &c. Apparently uted for 
sheep in general. 

Arth, p. 182. The hearth. 

AscENDENDO, 90, &c. This word must 
be taken in connection with Descen- 
DENDO, its opposite, terms which are 
in this book applied to the wages of the 
priests ofiiciating in the chantries of 
Bishop Hatfield, the two Nevilles, and 
Robert Rodes, within the cathedral. 
The Bursar^s Bool for 1508 aifords an 
explanation of the distinction and of 
the meaning of each word. " Descen- 
dendo ad inferiorem " is the entry in 
the book forthatyear. It would there- 
fore appear that when the word descen- 
dendo is used, the service at the begin- 
ning of the year, immediately after 
Whitsuntide, was undertaken by a 
monk of superior standing, who was 
succeeded, month after month, by his 
juniors in their respective order ; and 
that the term asceiidendo implies the 
converse of this arrangement, the 
junior monk beginning and giving 
place to his immediate senior, who 
passed the duty on to the man above 
him, and so on, till the end of the 
year. 

AssERES, 85. Spars of wood for the roof 
of a house, or perhaps occasionally 
laths. As they were obtained from the 
log of timber, not by sawing (sar- 
racio), but by tearing or splitting 
(laceratio), it is clear that boards 
eannot be meant when this word is 
used. 

AvENARius, 328. The keeper of the 
oats. 

AucA, 233. Notwithstanding the mo- 
dem usage of the word, as exclusively 
applicable to the puffin, it is clear that 
auca is here used for a goose.

LuDi Pbioris, 9, 13, &c At four pe- 
riods of the year, at the Feast of tiie 
Purification, at Easter, on the day of 
St. John the Baptist, and on the day of 
AU Saints, the Prior withdrew from 
Durham to one of his Manor hooses, 
attended by his officers and a consider- 
able number of the fratemity, for the 
purpoees of feasting and relaxation. 
The places to which he retired in their 
tums were generally Bearpark, Byew- 
ley, Pittington, and Wardley, but Mug- 
gleswick and other places were perhaps 
occadionally visited, and, in some in- 
stances, after the ordinaxy JMms had 
been brought to a termination, special 
lAidi were held, which, as in the case at 
Byewley, in 1538, hereafter mentioned, 
lasted for three days in succession. If 
any opinion can be formed from the 
consumption of provisions on these 
occasions, the people of the neigh- 
bourhood seem to haye attended in 
great numbers, and in all probability 
one of the amusements which they 
were permitted to witness was an exhi- 
bition of miracle plays or mysteries. 
The word Ludm seems to justify this 
inference. The ordinaiy allowance of 
provisions for these games made by the 
Bunar in each year is detailed in the 
pages above referred to, but the later 
bodu afford much additional informa- 
tion. In 1538, the XiM^tttf of AII Saints 
was held at Beaurepaire, and that of 
St. John the Baptlst preceding at 
Byewley. Of the expenditure and 
consumption of food at this latter plaoe,

LudtLS is first mentioned.
